## 7. The Return of Festive Flashing Lights - Computerphile
**Channel:** Computerphile | **Views:** 30K | **Date:** 1 month ago | **Duration:** 17:54 | **ID:** skmqR_VMECQ
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=skmqR_VMECQ

### Transcript:
So, I thought um it would be good to delve back into some history this Christmas. And if you remember back 11 years ago, long-term viewers to the channel, you'll remember that I built a set of computerc controlled Christmas lights. Uh and there was lots of comments about I should have used an Arduino rather than a Raspberry Pi. But let's let's bring these up to date and do them in 2025 rather than 2014. So last um last year, so last decade um I built these and I used individual leads. They're white leads and I had a wire for each of them that I then connected up to the Raspberry Pi. I didn't use an Arduino, much to the disgust of everyone in the comments, but hey, I use what's hand. And it had one wire plus ground for every single lead that was there. It took me forever to build it. I used old network cable to create the wires, insulating tape in green so it blended into the Christmas tree uh and so on. But I thought this year um I was putting my tree out at home. I thought why don't I do something similar but let's do it using 2025 technology. Actually let's do it using technology that was around if we're honest in 2014 and do it properly. And I'm going to use uh individually addressable LEDs um like the ones flashing away behind me. And I thought it'd be fun to talk about these because they actually the way they work, the way you program them, the way you send the data to them is quite fun and ties into some of the videos that people like Matt Godbolt have covered recently. So I think the place to start is to think about how these are connected up. When I built mine, I had, as I said, individual wires for each lead, which means I could turn them on and off individually. But if you look at these individually addressable LEDs, you'll see that there's actually only three wires used. Power, and in the ones I'm using, that's plus 5 volts. ground which is used to return the current from it and you have a data pin which goes to each lead and the way these are connected up is really quite straightforward you have your LED um and there's actually a driver chip which is driving these um we'll get into detail of that a bit later on and each individual lead has the power go into it the data go into it into the ground and then out of that LED um comes the power again the data which is then connected to the next LED And then that's all connected to the next LED and so on until you've run out of LEDs. The ones I've got at home have got 50 on. I think there's 100 ones behind me. So there's immediately a difference between how they're connected up.
